TO

DATE: April 9, 1947
FROM : SPD - Mr. Hibbard RE
FW 894.
SUBJECT:
Reference is made to the underlying draft letter to
Mr. Borton of the Office of International Trade Operations
concerning baggage regulations to be applied in the case
of Japanese nationals who may depart from the United States
11/2-647
for return to Japan at their own expense. It has been re-
drafted to include the information contained in CINCFE's
cable of March 20, 1947 regarding military regulations
respecting the importatin of motor vehiclesito Japan by
returning Japanese nationals.
It is not considered necessary to include in the
letter under reference the information contained in the
last sentence of the above-mentioned cable. This informa-
in
tion, however, will be included in statements regarding
baggage regulations which will be made available to the
interested Japanese nationals.
If the underlying draft letter is satisfactory to
JK, it would be appreciated if it could be initialed and
sent on to CR.

Attachment.

In the absence of Mr. Thomas (OIT) Miss Wolberg called
Miss Kennedy and made reference to several previous tele-
phone conversations with her and with Mr. Thomas on the
above-mentioned subject. Miss Wolberg explained that the
letter which SPD had proposed to send to Mr. Borton (Direo-
tor, OIT) had been heid up in the Department pending the
receipt of information from SCAP as to whether or not Jap-
anese nationals would be permitted to bring motor vehioles
into Japan. She stated that the necessary information had
been received and that the Department's letter would be
forthcoming.
Miss Wolberg then inquired whether Comprehensive Ex-
port Schedule No. 23 had yet been published. Miss Kennedy
informed her that it had been held up but that it should
come out shortly. She also mentioned that there had been

she had sent to Miss Wolberg on February 1lth. The changes,

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
TELEPHONE
Memorandum of/Conversation
DATE: February 19, 1947.
SUBJECT:
Baggage Regulations for Japanese Nationals,
not in an internee or a deportee category,
traveling to Japan at their own expense.
PARTICIPANTS:
Mr. Thomas, Office of International Trade, Department
of Commerce
Miss Kennedy, Office of International Trade, Department
of Commerce
111/2-647
Miss Wolberg, SPD
COPIES TO:
ese
1-1498
Miss Wolberg called Mr. Thomas and made reference to
several previous conversations on the subject mentioned
above. She stated that the draft letter to Mr. Borton which
Mr. Thomas had previously cleared by 'phone had been sent
back to her by the Division of Japanese and Korean Economio
Affairs with a memorandum to the effect that it is con-
sidered inadvisable, on account of the economic status of
Japan, to permit Japanese nationals to take motor vehicles
with them to Japen. She asked whether CIT would have any
objection if the State Department were to inrorm interested
Japanese nationals that they would be subject to the baggage
provisions set forth in the general license for personal
FW 894.1
baggage and effects (as set forth in Comprehensive Export
Schedule No. 23) with the following exception: that they
would not be permitted to take motor vehicles with them.
Mr. Thomas said that he could see no objection to this and
again the subject arose of the administration of these
baggage regulations in the case of returning Japanese. It
S
was agreed that it would be extremely difficult for the
customs collectors to apply them in each instance and that
if the State Department were to give the information to the
interested Japenese the latter, even if permitted by the
LIP UmI
collectors
47
291

=== Page 14 === (confidence 0.96)
-2 -
collectors to take with them items not permitted under the
reference regulations, would do so at the risk of having
them confiscated upon arrival in Japan. In view of the
foregoing, Mr. Thomas suggested that our letter be worded
in such a way as to leave it to the discretion of the OIT
whether or not special instructions should be issued to the
customs collectors.
Miss Wolberg then called Miss Kennedy and asked whether
Comprehensive Export Schedule No. 23 had yet been issued.
Miss Kennedy stated that the expected date of issuance had
been pushed up to about March 1, 1947.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE

SPECIAL/DIVISION
MEMORANDUM FOR THE FILES
894
February 6, 1947
On January 29, 1947 Miss Wolberg, SPD called
Mr. Thomas, Speciai Assistant to the Director of the
Commodities Branch, Office of International Trade Opera-
tions, Department of Commerce concerning baggage regula-
tions to be applied in the case of Japanese nationals,
2
not in an internee cr a deportee category, who may be
2-647
permitted to depart from the United States for return to
Japan at their own expense. Miss Wolberg informed Mr.
Thomas that the military authorities in Japan had indica-
ted that the above-mentioned Japanese nationals would be
permitted to bring into Japan only those items which
could reasonably be assumed to be for their own personal
use, including necessary household goods and such articles
as might be necessary for the proper execution of profes-
sional duties.
Mr. Thomas and Miss Wolberg agreed that, as in the
case of the German nationals of a similar category who
have been permitted to travel to Germany, the provisions
of the general license for personal baggage and effects,
as set forth in Current Export Bulletin no. 297 and re-
vised in Current Export Bulletin no. 338, should be applied
C
to the Japanese. Miss Wolberg informed Mr. Thomas that a
S
letter would be forthcoming from the Department of State
V
to confirm this agreement.
On February 6, 1947 Mr. Weymueller, American President
Lines informed Miss Wolberg, during the course of a conver-
sation concerning a related subject, that according to in-
formation he had received from Miss Kennedy, OIT a more
recent revision of the general license had appeared since
894.
the issuance of Export Bulletin no. 338. Miss Wolberg
checked with Miss Kennedy and found that there had indeed
been several revisions, some of them affecting the regula-
tions with respect to export of motor vehicles. The lat-
est revisionaprovides that the certification previously
required 1s no longer necessary. Miss Kennedy very kindly
N
0
11P Uut
offered
4
5972

=== Page 16 === (confidence 0.96)
一2
offered to send Miss Wolberg 25 copies of a summary of
the latest regulations under the general license, indicat-
ing that she would also be sending a copy to PD. With
respect to the letter which Miss Wolberg was drafting to
Mr. Borton on this subject, Miss Kennedy suggested that
the general license under reference be desoribed as that
which 1s set forth in Comprehensive Export Schedule no. 22,
as amended.
Miss Wolberg called Mr. Thomas to clear with him the
proposed change in the draft letter to Mr. Borton and he
suggested that the general license be described as that
which was set forth in Comprehensive Export Schedule
no. 23 in as much as the latter would be published on
February 15, 1947 and as it would contain the most recent
revisions of the license.

=== Page 17 === (confidence 0.96)
STANDARD FORM NO. 64
EPECANOROIFCTS
DATE: February 13, 1947
TO
: SPD - M. E. Wolberg
DXISION
FROM : JK - Mr. Martin
sujECT: Japanese Nationals Returning to Japan
89
This memorandun is in pursuance of your telephone conversation
of this date with r. Bowman of the JK Division. We are herewith
transmitting back for your reconsideration a proposed letter to Mr. John
C. Borton of the Department of Commerce, regarding package regulations
to be applied in the case of Japanese nationals who are not in an internee
or a deportee category and you may be permitted to depart from the United
States for return to Japan at their own expense.
2-647
We suggest reconsideration on the following grounds:
1. The second paragraph of your proposed letter appears not to
conform with the attitude of SCAP as indicated in their cable of 9 \November
1946. In that cable SCAP said "such shipment should contain only bona fide
household gcods as determined necessary and essential to the health and
comfort of the dependents and such other articles necessary for the proper
execution of professional duties." The cable also indicated that within
that limitation the shipments must also conform to Department of Commerce
regulations and theater directives. You will note that the general
licenset has three categories under the general heading of "Personal effects",
namely household articles, professional instruments and tools of trade, and
vehicles. We therefore think that it was not SCAP's intent to include
vehicles as permissible items of entry.
S
2. Even if we are incorrect with regard to SCAP's general position
V
we are inclined to feel that the economic status of Japan would hardly
justify returning nationals taking along such items as passenger vehicles.
The Japanese road system is not highly developed; heavy quantities of
oil and petroleum must be imported; and the contribution that automobiles
would at this time make to the recovery of Japanese economy is of dubious
value as against alternatives such as the Japanese national selling the
car here and making the dollar return available to SCAP.
Fw 894.111
We recognize that this is not an easy problem but we ask that
reconsideration be given and the Comerce Department be consult ed with a
view toward providing a bar to the transfer of such items as passenger
vehicles.
11P U
2-647
JK:DOBowman:bcb
Slux
2/13/47 (2959)

=== Page 41 === (confidence 0.99)
Form AD-1
Alien Departure Permit
Application No. 24128
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ALIEN'S APPLICATION FOR PERMISSION TO DEPART FROM
THE UNITED STATES
(For use by all aliens who desire to depart from the United States)
No fee prescribed
Send no money
This application must be executed in quintuplicate and sworn to before an officer
authorized to administer oaths. Each blank space must be filled in. If the space provided
is not suflicient to include the information requested, additional sheets of paper may be
utilized, with a reference to the question answered.
Five unmounted photographs of the applicant, recently made, not smaller than 2 by 2
nor larger than 3 by 3 inches, must be furnished with this application; one photograph to
be firmly affixed to each copy of the application in the blank space provided therefor. Each
photograph must be signed by the applicant in such manner as not to obscure the features.
An applicant registered or enrolled, or subject to registration or enrollment, for mili-
tary service in the United States must subnit with this application a statement from the
Secretary of War or from his local draft board stating whether there are any objectiqns to
his departure from the United States.
Other evidence may be required by the Secretary of State concerning the facts in the
applicant's case before a conclusion is reached on the application.
The receipt of the application will be acknowledged, and the applicant will be notified
later concerning the decision reached upon the application. If the permit to depart is
granted it will be forwarded to the appropriate authorities at the port through which the
applicant intends to depart from the United States, and the application number will appear
on a notification card which will be sent to the applicant. By calling upon the port author-
ities prior to or at the time of departure and presenting the notification card and identify-
ing himself as the person to whom permission to depart has been granted by the Secretary
of State, the applicant may be permitted to depart from the United States through the port
specified.
This application should be filed at least 30 days before the applicant intends to depart.
Any permit issued upon the basis of this application is subject to revocation at any time.
Every question must be answered in some manner, as no application will be acted upon
until complete information is furnished and unless it appears that the applicant has been
granted permission to enter the country of his destination and to pass through all countries
en route.
THE DEPARTURE CONTROL OFFICER WILL FILL IN THIS SPACE
Departed from

=== Page 49 === (confidence 0.99)
Form AD-1
Alien Departure Permit
Application No. 12
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ALIEN'S APPLICATION FOR PERMISSION TO DEPART FROM
THE UNITED STATES
(For use by all aliens who desire to depart from the United States)
No fee prescribed
Send no money
This application must be executed in quintuplicate and sworn to before an officer
authorized to administer oaths. Each blank space must be filled in. If the space provided
is not sufficient to include the information requested, additional sheets of paper may be
utilized, with a reference to the question answered.
Five unmounted photographs of the applicant, recently made, not smaller than 2 by 2
nor larger than 3 by 3 inches, must be furnished with this application; one photograph to
be firmly affixed to each copy of the application in the blank space provided therefor. Each
photograph must be signed by the applicant in such manner as not to obscure the features.
An applicant registered or enrolled, or subject to registration or enrollment, for mili-
tary service in the United States must submit with this application a statement from the
Secretary of War or from his local draft board stating whether there are any objectiqns to
his departure from the United States.
Other evidence may be required by the Secretary of State concerning the facts in the
applicant's case before a conclusion is reached on the application.
The receipt of the application will be acknowledged, and the applicant will be notified
later concerning the decision reached upon the application. If the permit to depart is
granted it will be forwarded to the appropriate authorities at the port through which the
applicant intends to depart from the United States, and the application number will appear
on a notification card which will be sent to the applicant. By calling upon the port author-
ities prior to or at the time of departure and presenting the notification card and identify-
ing himself as the person to whom permission to depart has been granted by the Secretary
of State, the applicant may be permitted to depart from the United States through the port
specified.
This application should be filed at least 30 days before the applicant intends to depart.
Any permit issued upon the basis of this application is subject to revocation at any time.
Every question must be answered in some manner, as no application will be acted upon
until complete information is furnished and unless it appears that the applicant has been
granted permission to enter the country of his destination and to pass through all countries
en route.
THE DEPARTURE CONTROL OFFICER WILL FILL IN THIS SPACE

Office Memorandum.
UNITED STATES GOVERNMENT
Mr. Williane
TO
SPD
DATE: February 14, 1947
Mr. Hibbard
FROM : SPD - MisS Wolberg
SUBJECT:
Reference is made to SCAP's circular no. 8 of
August 30, 1946 and in particular to paragraph no. 7
thereof concerning requests for authorization to enter
Japan. It is understood that in cases where an indivi-
duel whose government does not have a representative in
Japen desires to travel to Japan, the request from the
8
appropriate diplomatic mission at Washington will be
6
sent to the Military Permit Secretariat, Joint Chiefs
of Staff by SPD.
In as much as VD and PD both communicate with the
Military Permit Secretariat with respect to military per-
mits, I consulted with Mr. Meyer in VD and Mrs. Simpson
in PD as to their method of communication with JCs. There
is atteched a sample form (in triplicate) which VD uses.
Mrs. Simpson informs me that PD has no form--that each re-
4
quest is handled by a separate memorandum addressed to
Commander Millett with Mrs. Shipley's signature typewritten
(simply initialed by Mrs. Simpson). This memorandum does
not go through CR.
There seems to me to be no reason why applications
handled by SPD should go through CR and in the event of a
rush case clearance through CR is particularly inconvenient.
After speaking with Mrs. Flickinger in the Military Permit
Secretariat and being informed by her that all the informa-
tion listed in paragraph 7b of the above-mentioned circular
is necessary I do not believe that we can use VD's form.
Consequently you may wish to consider the underlying form
which I have drafted for possible use in this connection.
If it should appear that we are not going to receive a
large number of requests for permission to enter Japan, it
may be advisable to consider sending an office memorandum
to Commander Millett each time (not through CR) rather than
015
making up a form.
8
I found out that there 1s a daily messenger service
46
between VD and JCS and between PD and JCS and that no doubt

a similar0

there are enough requests to make it worthwhile.

SUBJECT:
Desire of Mercedes Ruiz-Huerta Horne and
Ana Segurola (Spanish Religious) and Rosa
Carola Gorini (Italian Religious) to travel to
Japan.
PARTICIPANTS:
Mrs. Flickinger, Military Permit Secretariat,

Miss Wolberg, SPD.

/2-1847
1—1408
Miss Wolberg indicated to Mrs. Flickinger that it
was possible that applications for permission for the
above-named Religious to enter Japan might already have
been submitted to SCAP by the Superior of their Order in
Japan. Miss Wolberg inquired whether the Military Per-
mit Secretariat would be informed if permission were
granted, and, if so, whether the Secretariat would inform
the Department or the interested individuals direct.
Mrs. Flickinger replied that the Secretariat would
doubtless receive the information and that it would be
forwarded either to Mr. Hutchison, VD or to SPD.
Miss Wolberg requested that SPD, rather than VD be given
any information which might be received from SCAP regard-
CS/A
ing these cases in as much as SPD is handling them.
Mrs. Flickinger said that she would be glad to inform
Miss Wolberg in the event that any information on these
three Religious should come through.

Mrs. McGranery called with reference to the applica-
tions submitted by the above-named nuns for permission to
depart from the United States for travel to Japan. (These
applications were brought to Mr. Hutchison, VD by a Miss
Davis in the Department of Justice and were then trans-
mitted to SPD by Mr. Hutchison. Miss Wolberg had informed
Miss Davis by telephone the previous day that the Depart-
ment proposed to inform the three nuns that in accordance
with the most recent regulations issued by SCAp with re-
spect to travel to Japan their requests must be sent to
their respective representatives at Tokyo for presentation
to the Supreme Commander. Miss Davis was also informed
CS/A
that, in view of the foregoing, the Department would advise
the nuns to communicate with their respective Embassies at
Washington requesting that their applications be handled in
accordance with the provisions of SCAp Circular No. 8 dated
August 30, 1946 entitled "Control of Entry and Exit of In-
dividuals, Aircraft and Surface Vessels into and from Japan.")
Mrs. McGranery was cbviously disturbed because of the
delay in the handling of the above-mentioned applications,
although she understood that the applications had only
recently
1847

=== Page 65 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
recently been received in the Special Projects Division.
Miss Wolberg informed her that the applications had been
received in the Visa Division at a time when a new pro-
cedure was being worked out for the handling of requests
of aliens desiring to travel to Japan. She explained
that it was only on February l4th that a waiver of the
exit permit requirement had been authorized in such cases,
that certain adjustments had had to be made under the new
procedure, all of which had helped slow down the action
in the three cases under reference.
Mrs. McGranery seemed satisfied that the delay had
been unavoidable but etated that she wished to do what-
ever she could to hurry these cases along as much as
possible. She said that she and the Judge felt respon-
sible for expediting the departure of the three nuns and
expla ined that they were going to Japan for the purpose
of teaching the children of American military personnel
and that the Navy had made all the necessary arrangements
for their passage. Miss Wolberg informed her that cer-
tain infornation should be submitted by the nuns to their
Embassles and Mrs. McGranery wished to know if she could
take down the information that would be required and have
the nuns write right away. She requested that the Depart-
ment communicate with her rather than with the nuns in the
matter. Miss Wolberg said that she would have to check on
this before giving Mrs. McGranery the information and
Mrs. McGranery said that she would call back in fifteen or
twenty minutes.
When Mrs. McGranery called again Miss Wolberg informed
her that the following information should be submitted by
each of the nuns to the appropriate Embassy:
1. Full name.
2. Place and date of birth.
Citizenship (if naturalized, date and place
of naturalization).
4。
Present occupation.

full description of nature of work to be

Memorandum of Conversation
TELEPHONE
March 31,
DATE: Axpxxixdxxix, 1947
FW
SuBJECT: Proposed travel to Japan of Senor Gonzalo de Ojeda, newly
appointed Spanish Minister to Japan, together with his
family.
89
PARTICIPANTS: Mr. Sanz-Briz, Second Secretary, Spanish Embassy.
Miss Wolberg, SPD.
COPIES TO:
2
1
-2147
Miss Wolberg called Mr. Sanz-Briz in reference to the Embassy
memorandum of March 21, 1947 requesting the Department to "forward the
documents permitting Mr. Ojeda and his family to enter Japan."
Miss Wolberg referred Mr. Sanz-Briz to the Department's circular
note of January 17, with which there were transmitted two copies of
Circular no. 8 of General Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powers, dated August 30, 1946 entitled "Control of Entry and
Exit of Individuals, Aircraft and Surface Vessels into and from Japan".
She referred in particular to paragraph no. 9 a (1) thereof which
states that upon arrival at point of entry in Japan an individual
must have in his possession "a passport or equivalent travel document
together with a statement in English from an authorized representative
of the country of his nationality at point of origin, that he has been
cleared by the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers for entry into
Japan; this statement will include the same information as required in
paragraph 7 b". Miss Wolberg indicated that if Mr. Ojeda and his
family did not possess the necessary written statements from the
Spanish Foreign Office that the Spanish Embassy at Washington would
seem to be the appropriate authority to furnish the statements. She
stated that no other papers or documents would be necessary to enable
Mr. Ojeda and his family to depart from this country for travel to
Japan. She then referred to section d of paragraph no. 9 and pointed
out that the necessary entry permits are issued at port of entry inte
Japan.
Mr. Sanz-Briz thanked Miss Wolberg for this information and re
quested written confirmation of it. Miss Wolberg said that a memorandu
tothe Embassy would be forthcoming.
894.111/2-2147

=== Page 100 === (confidence 0.99)
Mareh 3, 1947
Dean Edgar S. Furniss
Hall of Graduate Studies
Dear Dean Furniss:
As you know, this department wishes to continue the
program of oultural research in the Far East which was
interrupted by the war. Although conditions are unsettled
in the area, we are accustomed to carry on investigations
under such circumstances and particularly feel the obliga-
tion to do so now since the information is in immediate
demand by students being trained for various offieial and
private services. I am prepared to leave for the East by
June first, and the necessary funds are available. Rather
than return to Yunnan or go to Ssechwan, where my previous
Chinese associates are now working, I believe it to be most
profitable to direct our attention at this time to Korea
from which country two of our technically specialised
graduate students have recently returned, one having been
chief of the National Bureau of Culture (U. 8. M. G. in
Kores). As that part of Korea in which we wish to work
is under U. S. Military Government, it will be necessary to
have the cooperation as well as the permission of the State
and War Departments. Knowing your interest, as well as
that of President Seymour, in the development of Tale's
contribution to Far Eastern studies, I seek your adviee
in bringing about the effective relationships with govern-
mental departments whioh will result in advantages to all
concerned.
To carry out cultural research in Korea, with emphasis
during 1947 probably being centered on the indigenous
technology and its economic implications, I have sum-
marised the following arrangements which must be negotiated
for myself and possibly one other colleague or studont
assistant.
1. Official permission from the State and War Depart-
ments to conduct social soience researoh in the area of
Korea under U. S. control.
2. Trapsportation from a U. S. west coast port in
June 1947 and return in September, preferably by boat in
order to provide carriege of a reasonable amount of
equipment.
334

=== Page 101 === (confidence 0.98)
Dean Furnies - page 2
3. Cooperation of the Oooupation Government consistent
with the above mentioned approval for researeh.
4. Formal approval for round trip transportasion
from Korea to Nanking and/or Peiping, providing sueh srams-
portation is normally available, in order to consult with
colleagues in those cities in antisipation of my return
to China for a year's study in the near future.
On the attached sheet I have outlined information per-
tinent to an understanding of my own baokground. You may
be sure that I share your desire to move toward the solu-
tion of international problems and hope that through our
studies in the Far East we can make some contribution toward
that end.
Sincerely yours,
Cornolius Osgood,

SUMMARY OF DATA PERTINENT TO AR APPLICATION TO PURSUB
RESEARCH IN THE FAR EAST - 1947
Name: Cornelius Osgood.
Birth, Winchester, Mass., Maroh 20, 1905 (U. S. citisen).
Professional degree: Ph. D. in Anthropology, U. of Chieago, 1930.
Present position: Professor of Anthropology, Yale University
(also Direotor of Graduate Studies in Anthropology and
Curator of Anthropology in the Peabody Museum).
Previous travel and field research: First trip to China in
1935; study of Chinese village in Yunnan in 1938; aw elso
personally familiar with such adjacent countries as Japan,
Russia, and Indo-China. General field experienee includes
approximately three years field research in Aretic Canada
and Alaska, about one year in Venezuela besides shorter
periods of researoh in Ecuador, British Quiana, Brasil,
France, Spain, etc.
Language: Disclaim speaking knowledge of any language exeept
English, although, besides several European languages,
have had formal teaching in Korean, Classical and Modern
Chinese, and Russian.
Teashing: General and specialized courses in Anthropology for
seventeen years, including one on Chinese Culture and
Community Studios since 1942--all in the Graduate School,
Yale University.
Publications: Various monographs and papers resulting from
researoh mentioned above; unpublished manuscript on the
culture of a Chinese village.
Address: Yale University, New Haven, Connecticut.

the Department then refers the name or the applicant

had previously resided in Korea. I am mold, however,
that as living conditions improve, other persons
having definite interests in Korea will be given
permission to enter.

Mr. Ambassador:
IMENT OF STATE
at
I view of the fact that/the present time the
Government of Panama has no consular representation in
Yokohama, Japan, I shall appreciate it if Your Excellency
will be so good, if there is no objection, as to authorize
the consular official of the United States of America
in that place to provide an immigrant visa for the
passport of Mr. James G. Embry, of United States nationality,
contingent upon the presentation of certificates of good
health and conduct.
The person concerned, Mrs. Olga L. Embry, who has
made the said request of this Office, will present herself
at that Embassy with a copy of this official letter in
order to defray the expenses which this request may entail.
Please accept, Your Excellency, the assurances of
my highest consideration and esteem.
(signed)
R. J. Alfaro
Ricardo J. Alfaro
Minister of Foreign Relations
His Excellency
Frank T. Hines

re ENTRY OF FORMER BRITISH RESIDENTS INTO JAPAN
Sir/
I have to request that necessary action may be taken as
soon as possible ,with regard to the following----
I was for a number of years a British resident in
Hankow, China, but when the British Concession was handed back to
the Chinese in I927,I had no other alternative but to return back
to Japan, in which country I was a resident for many years,as a
school teacher.
In I940 I re-joined the British Navy,and was sent
from Japan to India,at government expense,together with my wife and
chijd ( wife is Japanese ) British by marriage.
I served in India with the Royal Indian Navy,until
1946, and was demobilized in Hongkong, June 1946, but was unable tos
C
enter Japan, this went on for nearly six months, and I thus lost the
sum of ten thousand Hongkong dollars,to say nothing of what I lost
in Japan,everything, cash,clothes,furniture and books.
Now, on the completion of my Naval service, I am unable
to return to Japan.
22
I have placed my case before the Chairman, of the
Conservative Party's Far Eastern Committee,House of Commons, London
with a request for immediate and effective action.
C
My present employment in Siam,is temporary only,
and I expect to complete my present work in June/ July,in which
month I shou'd desire to return to Japan,with my wife and child,if

=== Page 120 === (confidence 0.93)
2
you can obtain the necessry approval from the present administration
in Japan.
I served in the first world war of I9I4 - I919 with the
Royal Navy, so trust that some effective action may be taken with
regard to my case as soon as possible,in order that I may be abie to
proceed to Japan, in June / Juiy, via Hongkong and Kure.
I have a passage due me from the Government of
India,from Hongkong to Japan,also my wife and child.
I have the honour to be,
Sir,
Your Obedient Servant,
Malco1m W. Duncan.
Lieut Commander ( Retired )

Memorandum of/Conversation
DATE: March 26, 1947
SUBJECT:
Travel of Six Irish Sisters to Japan.
DIVISION OF
PROTECTIVE SERVICES
PARTICIPANTS:
Mr. Skentelbery, Irish Legetion,
ih
SEP.8.
1947
rs. Flickinger, Military Permit Secrettriat
xw
Joint Chiefs of Staff,
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Miss Wolberg, SPD.
/3-2647
COPIES TO:
1-1008
Mr. Skentelbery called to say that he had a new
cable from Dublin which stated that according to infor-
mation received from Tokyo by the Drishane Convent in
Ireland the above-mentioned sisters have been given per-
mission to enter Japan. The cable asked whether the
Legation here could confirm the foregoing information
and also asked whether each of the sisters might be per-
CS/V
mitted to take with her one ton of baggage.
Miss Wolberg then 'phoned Mrs. Flickinger in order
to ascertain whether a cable had been sent to Tokyo fur-
nishing the information with respect to the language re-
quirement set forth in the Department's letter drafted on
March 19. Mrs. Flickinger stated that the Department's ,
letter had been received only yesterday and that although
the cable had not been sent it would go immediately.
Miss Wolberg then asked what should be done about the
inquiry regarding the amount of baggage the sisters might
take with them. Mrs. Flickinger said that she would in-
clude in the cable an inguiry as to whether the sisters
might take with them a ton of baggage each. She said
that she would let Miss Wolberg know as soon as an answer
3
was in from Tokyo.
-2647
Miss Wolberg
18x

=== Page 127 === (confidence 0.95)
- 2 -
Miss Wolberg then telephoned Mr. Skentelbery to tell
him of the action taken. He stated that in as much as
it would probebly be a week and a half or two weeks before
an answer wes received from Tokyo he was going to send an
interim cable to Dublin. With respect to the desired oon-
firmation, Miss Wolberg stated that unless the sisters
actually had received from Tokyo some paper or document
from SCAP indicating that they had received permission to
enter Japan, they would really need the confirmation from
the Military Permit Secretariat. She said she would inform
Mr. Skentelbery as soon as an answer was received from
Tokyo.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Memorandum of Conversation
DATE: Apr1l 3, 1947.
Fil
Central
This Document Must Be Returned to
SUBJECT:
Travel to Japan
日
PARTICIPANTS:
Mr. Hibbard, SPD
Mr. Williams, SPD
Mr. W.S. McCornick, JK
COPIES TO:
8
394.111/4-347
ere
1-1488
After several telephone talks Mr. Bowman of JK
arranged a meeting for an exchange of ideas between
SPD and JK on the matter of travel to Japan and SPD's
duties under SCAP circular No. 8 specifying the
Department as the appropriate diplomatic channel for
the submission to SCAP of applications to enter Japan
originating with nationals of countries not having a
representative at Tokyo.
Mr. McCornick of JK met with Messrs. Hibbard and
Williams in the offices of SPD on April 3, 1947.
The immediate point under discussion was the
underlying draft radio to SCAP which SPD had composed
to clear up an apparent inequity in the treatment of
applications of U.S., as opposed to alien, businessmen.
Neither Mr. Lory of NA nor Mr. Bowman of JK had been
able to see clearly why SPD in handling businessmen's
applications submitted by foreign governments, could
8
not refuse to tranemit them to SCAP on the ground that
general SCAP policy prohibited entry to Japan of
S
private businessmen. American businessmen are
summarily refused at Washington.
60
Messrs.
14-347
mo

=== Page 139 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
Messrs. Hibbard and williams spent some time
explaining to Mr. McCornick the representation function
and the long-established pattern of inter-governmental
activity that SPD is trying to protect.
Mr. McCornick appeared to understand clearly SPD's
position that it could not properly judge the content
of something presented on an official basis by another
government for transmission to a third power (which
SCAP 1s in technical fact).
During the talk Mr. Bowman called from JK to state
that a newly received telegram from Tokyo made it olear
that SCAP insisted on the application of Ciroular 8
precisely as written.
It was then agreed (1) that the draft radio (asking
for revision) not be sent to SCAP; (2) that SPD would
handle requests presented by other governments for
services under Circular 8 in accordance with standard
SPD representation practice; (3) that SPD in doing so
would (as in the past) make every effort to keep all
Folitical, Economic and Control Divisions fully informed
of the traffic being handled.
SPD:EwiJliams III:erk
4/7/47

There is attached a draft letter to Mr. Borton

including the current provisions of the General
License for Personal Baggage and Effects (please see
memo of conversation of April 2) and also information
concerning regulations of the military authorities
in Japan regarding motor vehicles. I spoke with
Mr. Meyer, VD about how this information, as well
894.111/4-447
as tne necessary information regarding funds and
vaccinations, should be made available to the
interested Japanese. It was tentatively agreed that
VD, when notifying the Japanese that their exit
permit applications have been approved, can enclose
with their letters three mimeographed sheets containing
the necessary information with respect to (1) baggage,
(2) funds and (3) vaccinations. Mr. Meyer said that
there are pending in VD at least a couple of hundred
applications from interested Japanese and that he
believes at least 500 sets of regulations should be
made up. Do you think we can handle this mimeographing
job and make tne form statements available to VD to
be enclosed with VD's letters to the individuals?

REA
MEMORANDUM FOR THE FILES:
pun
Reference the attached file concerning
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
travel to Japan of Mother Mercedes Ruiz-Huerta Horne and
8
Mother Ana Segurola (Spanish nationals) and Mother Rosa
Carola Gorini (Itelien national). Judge James P.McGranery
of Philadelphie, Penneylvanie is interested in expediting
action on these cases and Miss Wolberg has on several
occasions spoken with his wife and his secretary, Miss Mary
Ethier, in the matter. During the past few weeks Miss Ethier
has mede severel trips to Washington and each time has tele-
4-1547
phoned to clear up various questions on these cases. (It
will be noted from the file that Mrs. McGranery was advised
that the cases should be handled by the Spanish and Italian
Embassies at Washington and the Spanish and Italian repre-
sentatives at Tokyo, that the Department of State does not
come into the picture at all.)
About ten days ago it was learned from Miss Ethier
that the Spanish Embassy had received word from the Spanish
representative at Tokyo that the tvo Spanish nationals had
been cleared for entry into Japan. Miss Ethier had also
checked with the Italian Embassy, however, and had learned
thet there had been no reply to two cables which the Embassy
had sent to the Italian representative at Tokyo requesting
that permission be obtained for Mother Rosa Carola Gorini
to enter Japan.
C
S
On Monday, April 7, Mrs. Flickinger (Military Permit
Secretariat, Joint Chiefs of Staff) called to say that a
cable just received in that office stated that SCAP had
granted permission for all three of the nuns under refer-
ence to enter Japan. In view of the procedure outlined in
SCAP s Circular No. 8 and as the Spanish Embassy had already
received clearance for the two Spanish nuns, it was decided
to do nothing further in the matter until later in the week.
It was hoped that by that time the Italian Embassy would
have received a reply from its representative.
On
ED
13.AP.T.

=== Page 147 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2-
On Monday morning, April 14, Mr. Martinez of the
Italian Embassy informed Miss Wolberg that still no reply
had been received from the Italian representative at Tokyo.
In as much as the three nuns wished to leave for Japan to-
gether with a fourth nun (sn American national) and as per
mission for all but the Italian nun had been received
through the appropriate channels, it was decided that the
Depertment would inform the Italian Embassy by memorandum
that clearance nad been received in Joint Chiefs of Staff
for Mother Gorini's travel to Japan. Mr. Martinez stated
that on the basis of the Department's memorandum the Embassy
would make up the statement required under paragraph 9a of
SCAP's Circular No. 8. In deciding on this action consid-
eration wes given to the fact thet SCAP's clearances for
entry into Japan are valid for sixty days only. Although
Mrs. Flickinger states that the Military Permit Secretariat
here can grant extensions it would be preferable to have the
nuns get to Japan within the 60-day period and thus avoid
further complications. (On Apr1l 15, 1947 Mr. Martinez
called to say that a reply hed just been received from the
Italian representative at Tokyo--stating simply, however,
that he was again approaching SCAP regarding permission for
Mother Gorini to enter Japan.)
Attachment.

The Acting Secretary of State presents his compll-
ments to Their Excellencies and Messieurs the Chiefs of
Mission of countries the Governments of which are not
represented in Japan and has the honor to refer to the
Department's note dated January 17, 1947 concerning a
modified procedure for applying for military permission
to enter Japan. Partioular reference is made to the
last paragraph of the aforomentioned note which states
that the Department will undertake the transmission,
within the scope of the Supreme Commander's Ciroular
No. 8, of applications originating with any government
having no representation in Japan.
In order to facilitate the handling of cames in-
volving individuals who desire to travel to Japan, &
form application (Form DS-513) has been devised. There
894.111/4-2447
are enclosed, for the convenience of the interested
Missions, ten sets of these form applications.
Additional

=== Page 164 === (confidence 0.96)
- 2-
Additional sets may be obtained from the Speeial Projeets
Division of the Department. It is suggested that in each
case involving the proposed travel of an individual to
Japan, the application should be completed in triplieate.
The original and one copy should then be forwarded by the
Mission concerned to the Department (Special Projeots
Division) whioh will refer the application to the military
authorities in Japan through the appropriate channels.
The remaining copy may in eaeh instance be retained for
the files of the interested Mission.

=== Page 172 === (confidence 0.99)
NO-YONG PARK
A.M. AND PH.D. OF HARVARD UNIVERSITY
AUTHOR - LECTURER
AVISITING PROFESSOR OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
AT THE UNIVERSITY OF KENTUCKY
And a Special Lecturer on Oriental Problems at the Institutes of Inter-
national Understanding Under the Auspices of Rotary International
He Will Revisit the Far East Every Summer and Will
Return With First-Hand Information
To Tell About the Great Changes Which Are Taking Place Across the
Pacific, the Changes Which Will Shape the Course of Tomorrow's World!
"HE IS ALWAYS STIMULATING, INTERESTING AND PROVOCA-
TIVE AND VERY MUCH IN EARNEST." – NEW YORK TIMES

=== Page 173 === (confidence 0.98)
NO-YONG PARK, PH.D.
Born and reared in Manchuria, Dr. No-Yong Park received his Oriental education in China, Japan and a,
and his Occidental training in Europe and America. He completed his undergraduate work at the Univers of
Minnesota and his postgraduate work at Harvard University where he received the degrees of A. M. and PD.
While in college he distinguished himself by winning many literary and oratorical prizes in competition with Amean
students in their own language. He has made Far Eastern problems his life-study and has taught the subject a
number of leading universities, including the University of Minnesota, Western Reserve University and the Unir-
sity of Kentucky. He has likewise served for the past ten years as a special lecturer on Far Eastern Affairs athe
Institutes of International Understanding under the auspices of Rotary International.
He is the author of numerous articles and books, some of which have been highly recommended by the Book-
the-Month Club and literary crities throughout the country. His books include Making a New China, An Orienil
View of American Civilization and Chinaman's Chance, a delightful autobiography packed with humorous exper-
ences and wholesome Oriental philosophy of life. He has also written Retreat of the West, a challenging and provocativ
book of facts and propheey on the fate of Western Empires in Asia, a book which Pearl S. Buck writes "must be i
required reading for the White race." His latest book is entitled The White Man's Peace, which is an Oriental view
of the Occidental attempts at building a new order in Asia and the world at large.
It is, however, his delightful and penetrating leetures which have won him fame and popularity among millions
of his listeners. His Oriental background and Occidental training. his gift of expression and keen sense of Mark
Twain-like humor, his foreeful. dynamie platform personality and his profound knowledge of Far Eastern affairs
have made him one of the most widely acclaimed Oriental lecturers in America. Year after year he returns to scores
of colleges and universities, conventions, lecture forums, men's and women's clubs.
SELECTED LECTURE TOPICS
“THE DAWN OVER ASIA"
“AMERICA'S ROLE IN TOMORROW'S CHINA"
“AMERICA AND RUSSIA IN ASIA"
“FOUNDATIONS OF PACIFIC PEACE"

STATEMENT
Christian missionaries will be oleared for entry into Japan
1f the following assurances oan be given:
(1) that shelter adequate for their use is owned
or is available for lease in Japan. (These assuranoes
should be based on actual inspections made in Japan to
determine the availability or the property involved.
Assurances may not be made on the basis of property which
is under requisition by the United States Army. However,
upon application by appro riate reprosentutives considera-
tion wiil be givon, suh ject to tho denands of military
necessity, to the retur of property to its owners in order
that it y e used s the basis for assura.ces.)
(2) that fona md clothins in adequate quantities
are available in Japan fron the resources of the organiza-
tions supporti.- tho missionaries for whon clearanoe is
sought, or that arrangements will be made for the
supplementary shipment to Japan of food and clothing to
be used exclusively for the support of such missionaries.
An organization which has no missionaries in Japan to give
the required assurances may send a representative missionary
there with the understanding that the representative will be
temporarily supported froa United states Army souroes on a re-
imbursable basis until he or she can become self-sufficient.
Such a ropresentative will bo expected to conduct the actual
inspections and surveys in Japan necessary for the provision of
the assuranoes required in connection with the subsequent entry
of operating missionaries.
When tho conditions respeoting shelter, food and clothing
for its missionaries have been met, the organization making the
required assurances (or its representative) should simultaneously
request authorization to ship to Japan any food, clothing or
other immediate necessities i tended for the use of its
missionaries. These shipments should be made in addition to the
normal amounts of baggage and household goods and must be
sufficient to prevent the missionaries conoerned from becoming a
charge upon the occupation forcos. The shipments should also
accompany the interested i .dl viduals in so far as that may be
practicablo. However, after the arrival of operating missionaries,
requests will be honorod fro: duly desicnated representatives of
various organizaticns and sects to authorize the shipment of
additional focd, clothin and other necessary supplies. Such
shipments may not exoeed one ton per individual per annum. Re-
quests for additional special shipments which are too large for
international mall will be considered on their own merits. If
authorization to ship household goods is desired, the request for
such authorization should be made at the time the necessary
assuranoes

=== Page 183 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
assuranoes are iven in connection with the request for per-
mission to enter Japan.
Use of Unitod States Arny supplies and faoilities will
ordinarily be extended to operating missionaries only in oases
of emergency in order to prevent extreme hardship and suffering.
Such use of United States Army supplies and facilities will be
extended to missionaries of any nationality on a reimbursable
basis and only to the sa e extent and on the same basis as suoh
privileges are available to other individuals of the same
nationality. Ho ever, upcn the arrival of operating missionaries
in Japan minimum assistance, if necessary to prevent hardship.
will be furnis ed the on a reimbursable basis. Transportation
will be arranged for them only fro. the port of debarkation to the
rail station nearest their ultinate destination
It is, or course, preferablo that missionaries sent to Japan
at this time be persons who have previously served there. However,
missionaries whc have had no prior service in Japan may be oleared
if they are to be associated directly with missionaries who are in
Japan and who have had prior service in that country. Assuranoes
must be given that those without prior service in Japan possess a
working knowledge of spokon Japanese, or that by the nature of
their work no language qualification is necessary.
No disorimination will be made among bona fide missionaries
on account of sex or nationality. Dependents of missionaries will
not be admitted unless the aro bona fide missionaries in their
own right.
Special visits by hi h officials in the organization or
hierarohy of any church will be considered on their own merits
as an exception to tho policy set forth above.
Cases involving islunds in tho vicinity ef Japan and
missionaries other than Christian will be roviewed and considered
on their own merits.

Miss Wolberg called Mr. Eichler in reference to Rangoon's
telegram 393 of May 15 on the above-mentioned subject and asked
whether invitations had been issued for various governments to
send into Japan reparation and restitution missions. Mr. Eichler
replied that SCAP had invited member governments of the FEC to
send such missions to Japan. He further stated that if the
Burmese Government were sending a representative to Japan in
this connection, that representative would have to be accredited
to the British Reparations and Restitutions Mission and would go
as a member of that Mission (he said that the British were
SS
allowed 25 members on their mission). Miss Wolberg later called
Mr. Eichler and cleared with him the draft of a cable to Rangoon
A
in reply to Rangoon's 393 of May 15.

SUBJECT:
Desire of Chinese Embassy to obtain Transit Permit
Through Japan for Mrs. Mei Li Tong Chen, Together
with her daughter and son.
PARTICIPANTS:
Commander Millett, Military Permit Secretary
894.
Joint Chiefs of Staff
Miss Wolberg, SPD
REH
SPECIAL PROJECTS
COPIES TO:
DIVISION
5
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
2
947
In answer to Miss Wolberg's inquiry, Commander Millett
stated that his office, upon receipt of a memorandum from
SPD, would be glad to issue a transit permit through Japan
for the above-mentioned Chinese nationals. He said he saw
no point in having them apply through the Chinese repre-
sentative at Tokyo in view of the time element involved.
During the course of the conversation Commander Millett
mentioned a telegram received in his office today from SCAP
recommending that American Consuls in various cities through-
out the world be authorized to issue transit permits through
Japan to American nationals. Commander Millett seemed to
ZW. 894.
think that a similar recommendation would be forthooming
from SCAP with respect to transit permits through Japan for
aliens. (He indicated that his office is fully authorized
S
to issue transit visas through Japan in a case like this
one.)

July 16, 1947
MEMORANDUM FOR THE FILES:
Reference the underlying despatch No. 3148 of
June 4, 1947 from Budapest transmitting the request of
FW
Mrs. Maria Dajkovich, a Hungarian national, for permis-
sion to enter Japan.
In view of the fact that the Supreme Commander at
Tokyo appears to have granted clearance for Mrs. Dajkovich
to enter Japan and to have notified the Military Permit
Secretary, Joint Chiefs of Staff, Washington (See enclo-
sure to despatch), Miss Wolberg telephoned Mrs. Flickinger
111/6-447
in Captain Keough's office, Military Permit Section,
Civil Affairs Division, War Department (formerly Miiitary
Permit Secretariat, Joint Chiefs of Staff) to check on
the matter. Mrs. Flickinger stated that on January 23,
1947 the Military Permit Secretary had sent a memorandum
to Mr. Meyer, VD, indicating that clearance had been
granted for Mrs. Dajkovich to enter Japan and suggesting
the appropriate Hungarien authorities be notified.
Miss Wolberg checked with Mr. Hutchison and Mr. Meyer,
VD, and with VD's file room only to be informed that VD
has no record of the above-mentioned memorandum of Janu-
ary 23. A check with DC/R likewise reveals no correspon-
dence concerning .aria Dajkovich. In view of the fore-
going, Miss Wolberg requested the Military Permit Section
(Mrs. Flickinger's secretary) to send over a copy of the
memorandum of January 23. Further action will be taken
on this case upon receipt of the memorandum.

894.
General understands that the Society of Mary with
headquarters in Dayton, Ohio, plans to make application
to the Department for a military permit for Mr. Edward
Sandrock, French, to travel to and reside in Japan for
the purpose of teaching in St. Joseph's College, Bluff
11/6-647
APPLN
SPI
85, Yokohama, Japan, which institution is understood
to be operated under the auspices of the Society of Mary.
CO:
TO.
In considering this application on behalf of Mr.
Edward Sandrock it is believed that the following informa-
tion, which has been obtained from a confidential source
Within the Government, will be of interest to the Department:
*Relative to Edward Sandrock, from information
CCO
gathered locally, he should be permitted to return
to his mission post in Japan. He has no Nazi or
Fascist tendencies. The reason for his leaving
Tsinan is that no American brothers of th Society
110
of Mary (Marianists) are available for Li Ming
CS/ JE C
School in Tsinan. Li Ming School will henceforth
be conducted by Chinese brothers of this order.
"Sandrock spent the majority of his missionary
life in Japan and as an old gentleman nearly 70, I
feel it would be wise to allow him to spend his
remaining years in the school to which he gave the
best years of his life."

Washington, June 21, 1947
POIC AFFAIRS
1947
Mr. Secretary:
I have the honor to address Your Excellency in order
to request that you be good enough to consider the possibility
that, through the intermediary of the Occupation Authorities
in Japan, the native Argentine citizens, Alfonso Sumio
Fushimi and Julio Hideo Fushimi, who are in Takamatsu,
Province of Kagawa Ken-Kasuga-Cho No. 1353, be permitted
to leave that country.
Once the departure of the aforementioned persons is
authorized, my Government will assume responsibility for
whatever expenses their transportation to the Argentine
Republic may involve.
Thanking Your Excellency for any steps which may be
taken in order to authorize their departure as requested,
I take pleasure in renewing to Your Excellency the assurances
of my highest and most distinguished consideration.
/Stamp of the Argentine
/signed/ 0. Ivanissevich
Embassy, Washington/
Oscar Ivanissevich
Argentine Ambassador

Winthrop G. Prown, Chief
Division of Commercial Policy
Department of State
Washington, D. C.
Dear Sir:
A number of my clients have asked me to ascertain the procedure to
be followed in applying for entry into Japan under the recently publio-
ized program for the resumption of trade with that country.
As you know, Hawaii has a very large Japanese population. Many
American merchants of Japanese ancestry here were engaged in commeroial
activity with Jupan prior to the war and have valuable contacts there
with which they are anxious to resume business. Furthermore, there is
a definite demand for Japanese made articles in the Territory.
I should greatly appreciate being advised as to the steps that must
be taken by persons wishing to participate in the new program and also
as to whether United States citizens only are eligible or whether Japanese
nationals in the United States with no record of subversive activity may
894.1
Xr
al so enter Japan.
Would you also be kind enough to forward any announcements, regulations,
application forms or other materials that might be of help?
611a.9431
As regular mail from the mainland is slow and as I am anxious to
secure this information at the earliest moment, I enclose air mail stamps.
Thanking you for any assistance you may be able to afford in this matter
and hoping for an early reply,
Yours very truly,
John F. Alefander
John F. Alexander
Office of Attorney General
Territory of Hawaii
Iolani Palace
Honolulu, T. H.

=== Page 291 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
COPY NO.57
25 June 1947
STATE-WAR-NAVY COORDINATING COMMITTEE
DECISION ON SWNCC 348
POLICY FOR THE INTERCHANGE OF PERSONS
BETWEEN JAPAN AND OTHER COUNTRIES
Note by
the Secretaries
1. At their 58th Meeting on 24 June 1947 the State-War-Navy
Coordinating Committee approved SWNCC 348 as previously amended
by SWNCc 348/1, subject to the qualifications in the enclosure to
SWNCC 348/3.
2. In approving the above paper, the Committee agreed not
to accept the amendment proposed by the Joint Chiefs of Staff in
SWNCC 348/2. In lieu thereof, the Committee agreed that the
United States Member on the Far Eastern Commission should be
directed to make a statement to the Commission as provided in
paragraph 4 of the enclosure to SWNCC 348/3.
3. The Committee also agreed to delete the cable proposed
by the State Member in SWNCC 348/3, and that the War Department
would take the matter up with General MacArthur directly.
H. W. MOSELEY

=== Page 294 === (confidence 0.99)
S E C R E T.
ENCLOSURE
POLICY FOR THE INTERCHANGE OF PERSONS
BETWEEN
JAPAN
AND
OTHER COUNTRIES
Report by the
State-War-Navy Coordinating Subcommittee for the Far East
THE PROBLEM
1. To determine United States policy with respect to the
interchange between Japan and other countries, during the period
of occupation, of peraons useful to the process of reorientation
of the Japanese people.
FACTS BEARING ON
THE PROBLEM
2. See Append1x "A".
DISCUSSION
3. See Appendix "B".
CONCLUSIONS
4.
It is concluded that:
a. During the period of occupation and subject to
the provisions of the following paragraphs there should
be permitted between Japan and other countries an inter-
change of persons for educational, religious, scientific,
informational and general cultural purposes including
members of trade unions and related organizations and
agencies whose purposes are recognized as democratic
and who will contribute towards the process of reorienta-
tion. Such interchange shall include participation by
the Japanese as well as other nationals in international
conferences and conventions, in either Japan or other
countries.
b. Each individual proceeding to or coming from
Japan must be sponsored. In the case of U.S. citizens
proceeding to Japan and Japanese nationals proceeding
to the U.S.A., the eligibility of each person and that
SWNCC 348
1-
Enclosure
(Revised 10 March 1947)
SECRET

=== Page 295 === (confidence 0.98)
S EC RET
of his sponsor should be approved by SCAP, the War Depart
ment and the Department of State. In the case of Allied
persons proceeding to Japan and Japanese nationals prooeed-
ing to countries other than the U.S.A., the eligibility
of each person and that of his sponsor should be approved
by SCAP and the country or countries of destination or
origin other than Japan, Communications regarding clearance
for suoh persons should follow established governmental
and military channels.
c. The total expenses outside of Japan, including travel,
living and incidental costs of Japanese going from Japan,
shall be defrayed by the non-Japanese nationals, institutions
or governments of the country or the countries receiving or
sponsoring such persons. The total expenses, including non-
yen equivalent of yen used in Japan, of persons going to
Japan shall also be defrayed by the non-Japanese nationals,
institutions or governments sponsoring suoh persons. Such
non-yen equivalent of yen used in Japan shall be made avail-
able in currencies usable by SCAP in payment for imports of
goods or services to Japan.
d. For the purposes of this program no interchange shall
be effected of persons who will engage in political or com-
mercial activities except insofar as these activities are
authorized by SCAP as contn buting directly to the accomplish-
ment of the objectives of the program.
e., Religious missionaries may engage in their normal
pursuits in Japan subject to the conditions stated in
Annexes "A" and "B" to Appendix "A".
f. Interohange of students, teachers and other personnel
engaged in social, natural, medical or other related scien-
tific studies, teaching or research shall be permitted pro-
vided such personnel and studies are of a non-military char-
acter and contribute to the process of democratization or
otherwise further the purposes of the occupation.
1
SWNCC 348
- 2
Enclosure
(Revised 10 March 1947)
SECRET

=== Page 296 === (confidence 0.98)
S E C R E T
g. The general program shall be prepared by SOAP in
consultation with the State and War Departments and with
the foreign governments concerned. Such a program for the
exchange of persons should take into consideration the
needs and objectives of the occupation and the conditions
and facilities available both in Japan and the countries
concerned.
RECOMMENDATIONS
5.
It is recommended that:
a. This paper be referred to the JCs for comment.
b. Provided the JCs have no objections from the military
point of view, this paper be approved by SWNCC and forwarded
to the State and War Departments for guidance; and that
thereafter
C. The conclusions be forwarded by the Department of
State to the U.S. representative on the FEC for submission
to that cody as a statement of the U.S. policy, and
d. The JCs be requested to forward the conclusions
to SCAP for his information.

one Japanese permitted to leave Japan for the United States.

ANNEX
"A" TO APPENDIX "A"
MESSAGE FROM GENERAL MACARTHUR TO THE WAR DEPARTMENT,
29 DECE-BER 1945
It is my policy to permit the return of missionaries to Japan
to the maximum extent practicable. It is proposed to approve the
return of all those missionaries who have previous service in
Japan as rapidly as applications are received provided that the
sect sponsoring their return can furnish assurance that it has
available suitable living accommodations and will provide main-
tenance which will guarantee that the individuals will not become
a burden on the occupation forces.
This policy is to apply
whether the missionary is male or female.
Under current economic directives the assurances of financial
support of
missionaries must be based entirely on church resources
in Japan.
Assurances of living accommodations mus: oe based on
investiation of local conditions by church representatives.
Assur nces of food must be based on arrangements between church
representatives in Japan and the Japanese Government.
Those
sects which at present have
no representation in Japan
are not
in a position to guarantee suitable conditions for reception of
their missionaries.
In or er to provide an equitable basis for
returning missionaries of all sects which had representation in
Japan prior to the war, it would be desirable for such sects not
presently represented here to return representatives qualified
to investigate local conditions and church resources available.
Authority would be needed to furnish such representatives
food, yen funds, housing, and transportation until they can
establish themselves, at which time they could assume responsi-
bility for giving this headquarters the necessary a? irances
on which return of other missionaries may be bas.i.
It is
believed that families should not be returned to Japan until the
present living conditions have materially improved.
This may
take place in the case of individual families progressively and
each case should be carefully examined on its merits.
SNCC 348
-6-
Annex "A" to Appendix "A"

The foregoing policy has been stated on the hasis of return-
ing missionaries who have actual experience here. It may be
extended to include new missionaries upon receipt of adequate
assurance of individuals, preparation to cope with the language
and other local problems of the country.

for lease in Japan for the use of missionaries for whom assurances
are given.
Such assurances shoula be based on actual surveys to
determine the availability of such property. Property which is
under requisition by the United States Army may not be used as
the basis for assurances; however, upon applicatio". by the proper
representatives consideration will be riven where military neces-
city permits to the return of property to its owners in order that
it may be used as the basis for assurances.
In addition to assurances required in respect to shelter,
further assurances must be given that food and clothing in ade-
quate quantities are available from church resources in Japan
to support the missionaries for whom clearance is sought, or that
arrangements will be made for supplementary shipment of food and
clothing to Japan which will be used exclusively for the support
of missionaries.

SECR.ET
APPENDIX "B"
DISCUSSION
1. The Potsdam Declaration states in part that "The Japanese
Government shall remove all obstacles to the rewival and strength-
ering of democratic tendencies among the Japanese people. Freedom
of speech, of religion, and of thought, as well as respect for
the fundanental human rights shall be established". To that end,
it would appear essential that the Japanese people be allowed to
resume contact at the earliest possible date with all democratic
peoples, the more effectively to expedite the reeducation of
Japan in the denocratic way of life.
2. An interchange of persons in the fields of education,
religion, science, information and general culture between Japan
and democratic countries will further Japanese reeducation in
the following respects:
a. It will assist the Japanese in overcoming their
nationalistic isolation in the spiritual and intellectual
spheres by the reestablishment of personal ties with
citizens of democratic countries.
b. It will provide an effective means of countering
the undermining influences of Japanese militarists and
reactionaries by enabling reliable Japanese to gain first-
hand knowledge of the ways of democracy in other countries.
c. It will assist in achieving the aims of the occupa-
tion by providing additional personnel for the reorienta-
tion of the Japanese people toward democracy and peace.
d. It will enable the citizens of democratic countries
to acquire direct appreciation and understanding of the
problems of Japanese reorientation and of the work neces-
sary for their successful solution.
3. Subject to the favorable development of Japanese re-
orientation, the interchange of persons should be conducted
increasingly on a private basis, in the educat onal, religious,
SWNCC 348
-11-
Appendix "B"
S E C R E T

=== Page 305 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
scientific, informational, and cultural spheres or in other areas
of importance for the spiritual and intellectual reorientation of
the Japanese people toward peace and democracy. Until such a
time, however, as interchange can be placed largely on a private
basis, SCAP, in consultation with appropriate U.S. governmental
agencies and with the foreign governments concerned, should have
the resoonsibility for formulating and executing a positive pro-
gram of interchange of persons which will provide the Japanese
with knowledge of the achievements of denocratic nations.
4. In selecting persons for the purposes defined under
paragraph 2, the following types of individuals shall be regarded
as particularly suitable:
a. Leaders in education--professors, teachers and other
persons concerned with educational affairs.
b. Leaders in religious activities, wherever they would
contribute to freedom of religion or the spread of other
democratic ideals.
c. Leaders in science--social, natural, medical and
ot er related sciences conducive to the development of a
peaceful ano democratic Japan.
d. Leaders in informational and related fields of
activity, such as press, radio and films.
e. Leaders in civic, welfare, youth, social and other
organizations.
f. Leaders in occupational and professional organiza-
tions, such as agricultural associations and representatives
of trade unions.
g. Leaders in art, letters, music and the stage.
h. Qualified students from institutions of learning,
trainees, and professional research experts.
5.
Approval of persons should be carried out as follows:
a. Certification must be made by each applicant that
his trip will not be utilized to serve commercial or
political interests.
The trip of each applicant must be
SWNCC 348
-12-
Appendix "B"

=== Page 306 === (confidence 0.99)
2
SECRET
sponsored by an organization, institution or an agency
cither official or private.
b. The total number of applicants for entry into Japan
should be limited by the nature and objectives of the control
machinery and the problem of living conditions in Japan.
c. In the case of U.S. citizens proceeding to Japan and
Japanese nationals proceeding to the U.S.A., the eligibility
of each person and that of his sponsor should be approved by
SCAP, the War Department and the Department of State. Such
approval shall depend on the results of a thorough security
check made by the appropriate a encies in Japan and the
United States, and submitted with full information on each
candidate to SCAP, to the War Department and to the Depart-
ment of State.
It shall be understood that, in case of
reasonable doubt regarding a candidate, his application
shall be disapproved.
i. In the case of Allied persons proceeding to Japan
and Japanese nationals proceeding to countries other than
the U.S.A., the eligibility of each person and that of his
sponsor should be approved by SCAP and the country or
countries of destination or origin other than Japan.
Such
approval shall depend on the results of a thorough security
check made by the appropriate agencies in Japan and in the
foreign countrles concerned and submitted with full infor-
mation on each candidate to SCAP.
It shall be understood
that, in case of reasonable doubt regarding a candidate,
his application shall be
disapproved.
6. Payment of Expenses.
SCAP should be responsible for
restricting yen funds of persons visiting Japan to amounts re-
quired for reasonable personal expenses.
In the case of United
States nationals, each person should submit for prior approval
of the War and State Departments, a certified statement of
sources of funds to cover his or her expenses.
The sponsoring
person, institution or government is responsible for making
SWNCC 348
-13-
Appendix "B"

=== Page 307 === (confidence 0.98)
SECRET
arrangements for all necessary expense payments. Yen may be pur-
chased by methods currently authorized (e.g., through Wer
Department, National City Bank, etc.). Currency other than
dollars, if usable by SCAP for purchases of imports or services,
may be accepted in payment for regular yen. "Type A yen" for
expenditure in Army Post Exchanges must be procured with dollars.
Any compensation received by Japanese nationals in the demo-
cratic country visited, over and above actual exper.s, shall
be exchanged for yen at the prevailing rate, anc the equivalent
chall be deposited in a special fund for cultural purposes.
This fund may be drawn upon to defray expenses involving cur-
rencies other than yen which are not covered by the sources
listed above.

Form AD-1
Alien Departure Permit
Application No.24718
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ALIEN'S APPLICATION FOR PERMISSION TO DEPART FROM
THE UNITED STATES
(For use by all aliens who desire to depart from the United States)
No fee prescribed
Send no money
This application must be executed in quintuplicate and sworn to before an officer
authorized to administer oaths. Each blank space must be filled in. If the space provided
is not sufficient to include the information requested, additional sheets of paper may be
utilized, with a reference to the question answered.
Five unmounted photographs of the applicant, recently made, not smaller than 2 by 2
nor larger than 3 by 3 inches, must be furnished with this application; one photograph to
be firmly affixed to each copy of the application in the blank space provided therefor. Each
photograph must be signed by the applicant in such manner as not to obscure the features.
An applicant registered or enrolled, or subject to registration or enrollment, for mili-
tary service in the United States must submit with this application a statement from the
Secretary of War or from his local draft board stating whether there are any objections to
his departure from the United States.
Other evidence may be required by the Secretary of State concerning the facts in the
applicant's case before a conclusion is reached on the application.
The receipt of the application will be acknowledged, and the applicant will be notified
later concerning the decision reached upon the application. If the permit to depart is
granted it will be forwarded to the appropriate authorities at the port through which the
applicant intends to depart from the United States, and the application number will appear
on a notification card which will be sent to the applicant. By calling upon the port author-
ities prior to or at the time of departure and presenting the notification card and identify-
ing himself as the person to whom permission to depart has been granted by the Secretary
of State, the applicant may be permitted to depart from the United States through the port
specified.
This application should be filed at least 30 days before the applicant intends to depart.
Any permit issued upon the basis of this application is subject to revocation at any time.
Every question must be answered in some manner, as no application will be acted upon
until complete information is furnished and unless it appears that the applicant has been
granted permission to enter the country of his destination and to pass through all countries
en route.
THE DEPARTURE CONTROL OFFICER WILL FILL IN THIS SPACE
Departed from
Date
Departure Control Officer.
(1)
16—25069-1

Iranian Embassy request for permission for

894.
Miss Wolberg, SPD.

1-1408
Miss Volberg called in reference to the Iranian
Embassy's note No. 1523 of July 2, 1947 regarding the
above-mentioned subject. She referred Mr. Aram to the
Department's Circular Note of April 24, 1947 with which
there were enclosed ten copies of Form DS-513 (Applica-
tion for Permission to Enter Japan). She pointed out
that the Iranian Embassy had not transmitted with its
CS/A
note a form application for Mr. Touty and that some of
the information required by the military authorities
when considering requests for permission to enter Japan
was not included in the Enbassy's note (such as place and
date of birth; final destination in Japan; number, date
of issuance, issuing authority and period of validity of
F0 894.111
passport). She indicated that time would probably be
saved in the end if, before bringing this matter to the
attention of the appropriate military authorities, the
Department could receive from the Embassy a fonm appli-
cation for Mr. Touty including all of the required
information.
Mr. Aram said that he would bring this matter to the
attention of the person who had drafted the Embassy's
note under reference and that Miss Wolberg would probably
hear from him in the very near future. Mr. Aram also
stated that Miss Wolberg's call could be considered an
nal.
acknowledgement to the Embassy's note.

SUBJECT:
Shipment of Food into Japan by Missionaries.
894.
PARTICIPANTS: Colonel Linvill, Civil Affairs Division,

Miss Wolberg, SPD.
DIVISION OF
111/7-847

File

wedd
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ePO
1-1408
In answer to Miss Wolberg's inquiry, Colonel Linvill
stated that missionaries traveling to Japan may take
with them any foods the export of which is not prohibited
by the Office of International Trade, Department of Com-
merce. He assured Miss Wolberg that the military in
Japan have placed no limitations on the amounts or types
of food which may be brought in, except that missionaries
are allowed to bring in a maximum of no more than one ton
of food each per year.

=== Page 377 === (confidence 0.98)
July 15, 1947
MEMORANDUM FOR THE FILES:
At Mr. Williams' instance, Miss Wolberg endeavored
to track down a rumor to the effect that SCAP had placed
a limitation on the amount of baggage which may be taken
to Japan by Japenese nationals, not in an internee or a
deportee category, who are returning to Japan at their
own expense for permanent residence. (SPD had previously
drawn up an "Information Sheet Concerning Baggage and
Effects" which is being made available to all Japanese
whose departure from this country for repatriation to
Japan is approved by the Department. The aforementioned
sheet was drafted with the idea in mind that SCAP had
placed no limitation on the amount of baggage which
these Japanese could bring with them, except that it must
be for their own personal use and that they must conform
with Department of Commerce export regulations. The
sheet was drafted after several exchanges of telegrams
between SCAP and the Department, and it was believed
8
that if SCAP had placed any limitation the Department
394.111/7-154
would have been informed in one of the incoming cables.)
Miss Wolberg called Mr. Allen, Water Transportation,
War Department (Extension 6652) who ssid he would check
on this matter, although he was under the impression
that the limit on the amount of baggage which the Japan-
ese could take was 4o0 pounds. He checked with Major
Bailey, Office of the Assistant Chief of Staff, G-4
(Extension 6l58) and called back to say there is no
limitation--that the 400-pound figure applies only to
547
the amount which may be taken by Japanese who travel on
Army vessels.
In order to make doubly sure, Miss Wolberg checked
with Major Bailey who assured her that, so far as he
knew, SCAP had placed no limitation on tho amount of
baggage these Japanese may take and that the information
89
sheet drawn up in SPD is probably quite correct.
15
S
ASPD:MEWolberg:rjt

Mr. Secretary of State:
I have the honor to address Your Excellency in order
to inform you that the Government of Uruguay wishes to
establish a commercial office in charge of a commercial
delegate in Japan.
To this end the Uruguayan Government nas appointed
(its) Consul, Mr. Carlos Hounie Fleurquin, Commercial
Delegate of Uruguay in Japan with residence in Tokyo.
In accordance witn the regulations adopted by the
Supreme Command of the Allied Powers in Japan, I beg
Your Excellency to have the kindness to communicate this
wish of the Government of Uruguay to the Supreme Command
of the Allied Powers, interceding, at the same time, so
that a decision be made favorable to the proposal of my
Government to reestablish commercial relations with Japan.
I take pleasure, likewise, in sending with the present
note Form DS-513, in duplicate, in which the corresponding
permit to enter Japan is requested for Mr. Carlos Hounie
Fleurquin, in accordance with the provisions of circular
No. 8 of the Supreme Commander of the Allied Powers in
Japan.
I salute Your Excellency with my highest consideration.
(signed) Jose A. Mora
