Miss Wolberg called Mr. Yriart in regard to the
Embassy's note of July 15, 1947 on the above-mentioned
subject. She informed Mr. Yriart that according to
information received from the American Embassy at
Montevideo, Mr. Hounie plans to have his wife accompany
confirmed that Mrs. Hounie, is to accompany her'husband
to Japan. Miss Wolberg pointed out that the Embassy had
not submitted an application for Mrs. Hounie and that
consequently some of the information normally required
by the military in Japan when passing on applications
for permission to enter that country is not available to
the Department. Mr. Yriart requested that Mr. Hounie's
application be forwarded to the military in Japan and
that SCAP likewise be informed of Mrs. Hounie's desire
to enter Japan. He said that he would try to get the
necessary information regarding Mrs. Hounie, but asked
that meanwhile the matter not be held up. Miss Wolberg
indicated that there was some chance that Mrs. Hounie's
application might be referred back to the Department for
lack of information. Mr. Yriart said he would prefer to
take that chance.
In connection with a statement from the American
plan

=== Page 386 === (confidence 0.95)
一2-
plan to leave Uruguay on August 8 fon the United States
in transit to Japan, Miss Wolberg thought it wise at this
point to inform Mr. Yriart of the fact that the military
permit people are overloaded and it might take some time
to get these applications through. Mr. Yriart stated
that he hoped this would not be treated as an ordinary
case in view of its official character. Miss Wolberg
assured Mr. Yriart that the Department would do everything
in its power to speed the matter up.

FROM : Mr. Williams
SUBJECT:
I talked with Bowman of JK this morning. He is wait-
ing for OK's from SCAP and USAMGIK before sending this
circular airgram out. He has promised to talk to us again
before letting it go.
He has accepted and written into his own draft our
correction of lines six and seven on page two.
VR
With respect to page six, paragraph one instructing
non-U.S. nationals to apply to USAMGIK through the Consulate
at Seoul, JK seems to be as much in the dark as we are.
895.111
He expects to be somewhat enlightened on the possibilities
8
of the situation by USAmGIK's reply to his request for
6
clearance of the material set forth in the draft circular
4.111/7-1647
8
airgram.
He is not at all unwilling to have requests channeled
through the Department after the manner of applications to
enter Japan, if that appears desirable.
There is some question in my mind as to whether it is
desirable from SPD's point of view. Principle may indicate
the desirability of clearing such applications through the
Department but SPD already has a growing work load on
Japanese applications and the Korean business might well be
more complicated, case by case, than the Japanese.
In any event, we are supposed to be kept informed. I
suggest that we make some effort to find out what we (State)
have in Korea and how much weight our outfit can be expected
to carry.
I also offered Bowman the idea that nothing could be
easier than splitting his airgram into two airgrams, since
Japan and Korea are separate subjects, if the matter worked
out so that consideration of the Korean problem was holding
up the Japanese.

DIVISION OF PROTECTIVE SERVICES
August 1, 1947.
FW
MEMORANDUM FOR THE FILES
894.111/7-1847
Mr. Williams called Mrs. Flickinger (Military
Permit Section, War Department) to discuss the issuance
by certain American consulates of military permits
authorizing the transit of Japan by aliens traveling by
air.
Mr. Williams referred to the CINCFE cable (ZX 49231
(MCN 51386) June 4, 1947) which authorized the adoption
of such procedure for Americans making short stops in
Japan while in an air transit stetus and which was thought
broad enough to cover similar treatment for aliens.
Mr. Williams stated that DS had now obtained a copy
of the cable and did not feel, after examining it, that
the language was broad enough to justify authorizing
American consulates to endorse alien passports for such
transit.
After some discussion it was agreed that the
00
Military Permit Section would cable Tokyo for assurance
on the point. Mrs. Flickinger promised to let Mr. Williams
know as soon as an answer came in.

yn

=== Page 402 === (confidence 0.98)

EMBASSY
WASHINGTON.D.C.
JSTON OF JAPANESE AND KOREAN
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
7/25
SUL251947
Dc/R
Dear Mr. Bowman:
fiee
As you requested in our telephone conversation of a
few minutes ago, I am quoting herewith the text of an immediate
teletype which we have just received from the Canadian Department
of External Affairs:
" Entry of Canadian business men into Japan.
1. In accordance with the provisions agreed upon
in the Far Eastern Commission for regulating the entry of business
894.111/7-2147
men into Japan (FEC 088 series and C2-288 series) many applications
have been received by the Department of Trade and Commerce from
Canadian business men wishing to enter Japan on or after August
15th. Five applications which have so far been accepted and
forwarded to us by that Department have in turn been forwarded to
the Canadian Liaison Mission in Tokyo for formal submission to
SCAP.
2. On June 28th a cable was sent from Tokyo with
text as follows:
'(1) General Headquarters officials state details
for entrance and lodging of business men not completed as yet.
This Mission will be informed when final plans are known.
Assurance has been given that such information will be forthcoming
in time to permit entry as predicted early in August.
(2) Under the present circumstances, Canadian
applications would be rejected. Therefore, they will not be
submitted until regulations favourable.
(3) There are indications that General Headquarters
plan to set up some billet arrangements along the lines of a
commercial hotel.'
3. We have not since had any communication from
Tokyo on this subject and we therefore presume that SCAP approval
has not yet been granted the five Canadian applications.
4. The Department of Trade and Commerce informs us
CS/V
that they have received urgent letters from certain of these
applicants stating that (a) they have had to cancel their boat
passages already, and (b) they have been given a time limit of seven
days by the airline to either pay for or cancel their reservations
for August 14th. If they cancel, they will be unable to reach
Japan before late September. If they pay for their passages but
are unable to go because of non-clearance by SCAP, they will forfeit
their passage money, and they have urgently requested guidance
from the Department of Trade and Commerce as to which course they
should follow.
AP UCR
/5. On July 19th
Mr. D. O. Bowman,
Acting Chairman,
Inter-Allied Trade Board for Japan,
Room 19, State Department,
17th and Penmsylvania Aves., N.W.,
Washington, D.C.

=== Page 403 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
5. On July 19th an immediate telegram was sent to
Tokyo requesting an indication of when we might expect to have the
applications approved by SCAP.
6. Unless action is taken in the very near future
by SCAP it will mean that not only will the business men in the
Canadian quota be unable to reach Japan in time to benefit from the
provisions for which we pressed in the FEC over a period of several
weeks, but it will also largely destroy the value of the work put
in by the FEC in reachithe agreements as to quotas and procedures."
It is my understanding that you will use this
teletype as the basis of a cable which you intend to send to SCAP
asking him to clarify this position as quickly as possible.
Yours faithfully
In. Beaupni.
T.N. Beaupre

On September 2, 1947, Miss Carson of the Iranian Embassy, called
to inquire regarding the status of the application of Hussein Touty,
an Iranian national, for permission to enter Japan.
On September 2, Miss Wolberg telephoned Miss Derouffee of the
Military Permit Section, War Department, to ask about this case. As
nothing had been heard from the Military Permit Section by the next
day, Miss Wolberg telephoned again and this time Mrs. Tracer promised
to let her know the status of the case. On September 5, Miss Wolberg
again telephoned and was informed by Mrs. Tracer that there appeared
to be no record of the case in the office. Miss Wolberg then spoke
with Miss Derouffee who said that she would be coming in the next day
(Saturday) and would make a thorough search for this case. Miss Wol-
berg called Miss Derouffee again on September 8 and was informed that
there was definitely no record of the case in the Military Permit
Section. Apparently the Department's letter of August 13,-on the case
was never received. Miss Wolberg then said that she would send a
copy of the Department's letter and requested that action on it be
hurried as the Iranian Embassy was going to be quite upset over the
delay. Miss Derouffee said that if the copy was addressed to her
Teliphoned Mis Carson, hanian Smtassy and ruprimed her
of the tatus I the case. ()

Form AD-1
Alien Departure Permit
Application No.247/8
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ALIEN'S APPLICATION FOR PERMISSION TO DEPART FROM
THE UNITED STATES
(For use by all aliens who desire to depart from the United States)
No fee prescribed
Send no money
This application must be executed in quintuplicate and sworn to before an officer
authorized to administer oaths. Each blank space must be filled in. If the space provided
is not sufficient to include the information requested, additional sheets of paper may be
utilized, with a reference to the question answered.
Five unmounted photographs of the applicant, recently made, not smaller than 2 by 2
nor larger than 3 by 3 inches, must be furnished with this application; one photograph to
be firmly affixed to each copy of the application in the blank space provided therefor. Each
photograph must be signed by the applicant in such manner as not to obscure the features.
An applicant registered or enrolled, or subject to registration or enrollment, for mili-
tary service in the United States must submit with this application a statement from the
Secretary of War or from his local draft board stating whether there are any objections to
his departure from the United States.
Other evidence may be required by the Secretary of State concerning the facts in the
applicant's case before a conclusion is reached on the application.
The receipt of the application will be acknowledged, and the applicant will be notified
later concerning the decision reached upon the application. If the permit to depart is
granted it will be forwarded to the appropriate authorities at the port through which the
applicant intends to depart from the United States, and the application number will appear
on a motification card which will be sent to the applicant. By calling upon the port author-
ities prior to or at the time of departure and presenting the notification card and identify-
ing himself as the person to whom permission to depart has been granted by the Secretary
of State, the applicant may be permitted to depart from the United States through the port
specified.
This application should be filed at least 30 days before the applicant intends to depart.
Any permit issued upon the basis of this application is subject to revocation at any time.
Every question must be answered in some manner, as no application will be acted upon
until complete information is furnished and unless it appears that the applicant has been
granted permission to enter the country of his destination and to pass through all countries
en route.
THE DEPARTURE CONTROL OFFICER WILL FILL IN THIS SPACE
Departed from
Date
Departure Control Officer.

AUG 13 1947
DIVISION OF
Washington, D. C.
Dear Sirs:
It is requested that the enclosed papers of Mrs. Ashman
be processed without delay for the following reasons:
Mrs. Ashman and I are scheduled to depart from San Fran-
cisco, September 3rd on the American President Lines, Marine
Leopard, for Bombay, India, and naturally desire to have her
re-entry permit before leaving the States.
-1147
Early in June, immediately after our arrival in San Fran-
cisco, Mrs. Ashman reported to the Immigration and Naturaliza-
tion Bureau, stating that she would be leaving the States
early in the Fall and desired to start her processing for
naturalization. She was told there was nothing she could
do until we return from abroad; at this time nothing was told
to her about departure or re-entry pennits.
When Mrs. Ashman told me atout her experience, I could
not believe it to be true, so I phoned the above Bureau and
was informed that the only thing Mrs. Ashman should do before
leaving the States was to obtain a re-entry permit.
On the strength of the foregoing, neither Mrs. Ashman
nor myself did anything about her re-entry permit until last
week when we received our steamship booking. When Mrs. Ashman
C
S
applied at the Bureau of Immigration and Naturalization, she
was treated very rudely by the clerk who waited on her. It
1
was only then that a departure permit was mentioned, and she
2
was told that before she could receive her re-entry permit,
the departure pemit must be processed in Washington and
Philadelphia, she being criticized for not submitting it sooner.
In view of the foregoing, we feel the blame for the tardi-
ness of submitting these papers does not rest with us, but
rather with the Bureau of Immigration and Naturalization,
and request that these papers be processed with the greatest
of haste.
Thanking you for your attention to this matter, I remain,
Very truly yours,
Gronge Z. Ashmm
8
Georgo L. Ashman
GLA:bf

him in VD has to do with his deportation from this
country on July 22, 1944; he was granted permiss1on to
re-apply for admission to the United States after depor-
tation when in possession of an appropriate visa.]

In accordance with the instructions contained in your Excellency's

sent to this Embassy the application submitted by Mr. Eduardo Azrak
(who was) born in Panama in 1917.
In view of the foregoing, I have the honor to send to you
Excellency, two copies of the application under reference with the
request that you will please inform me as soon as possible of the
decision reached regarding Mr. Azrak's request.
Thanking you in advance for the attention which your Excellency
will give to this matter, I express to you again the assurances of
my highest consideration.
J. J. Vallarino

MEMORANDUM FOR THE FILES
111/8-1347
A check with DC/R (Miss Chaney checked with DC/R's files from
1930 to 1939 and Miss Graham checked DC/R's files since 1940) and
with FC (Mr. Raley) reveals no information concerning Eduardo (Ed-
ward) Azrak.
Mr. Donovan, FC, checked in the economic security records
which were transferred last month from OE to FC. In the file on one
Annibal Vallarino he found a reference to Eduardo Azrak. (Annibal
Vallarino, a Panamanian national, was designated a Special Blocked
National on December 4, 1942 as the result of Treasury Department's
charges of suspicious activities of smuggling United States currency
and otherwise evading Treasury Department controls; in June 1944 he
became a Proclaimed List national. His name was eventually deleted
from the latter list merely as part of the over-all reduction of the
list and not by way of clearing him of the charges. Consequently, his
name was re-included in the Schedule of Special Blocked Nationals.
Subsequently, Vallarino was classified as a merit deletion from the
Proclaimed List.) The reference to Eduardo Azrak appears in a
Treasury Department(Foreign Funds Control) report dated October 11,
CS/A
1943 and states that Eduardo was born in Panama in 1922; he returned
to Syria in 1930; accompanied his brother, Alberto, to Panama in 1936;
in October 1943 he was engaged in a wholesale merchandise business
with Alberto, conducted in Alberto's name. In April 1943 Alberto and
Annibal Vallarino had become partners in the firm "Vallarino y Azrak
Linitada" and Eduardo was employed by the company; in June 1943 the
partnership was dissolved, apparently because Vallarino decided he was
not making enough profit.
F.W,894.11/8-1.
An examination of VD's file on Eduardo Azrak reveals that he is
stated to be a native-born Panamanian nationals, born on February 14,
1917. In despatch no. 1797 of May 28, 1945 the Embassy at Panama, in
MAY 19
reply to the Department's query as to why the Embassy had required
FILED
that sponsorship forms be completed in the case of Edward Azrak - then
a visa applicant -, stated that in December 1943 his name was checked
1948
with

-2-
with Intelligence Agencies in the Canal Zone "who stated that they
had nothing in particular against him but that he and his family
had been under suspicion as friends of Nazi sympathizers. EDWARD
AZRAK stated in his visa application that he was born on February 14,
1917, in Panama. In view of the recommendations at that time that
EDWARD AZRAK not be granted a visa for the United States without a
thorough investigation of his antecedents he was informed that he
would have to request two interested parties in the United States to
file sponsorship fcrms in his behalf. A subsequent visa clearance
received from G-2, PCD on October 27, 194l stated in part, 'It is
therefore recommended that Subject not be granted a visa, in view of
his relationship to persons of dubious ethics, and the strong pos-
sibility of being the representative of these persons.' According
to a cable intercept dated May 23, 1945 from ALBERTO AZRAK,Panama,
to EZRA AZRAK, Aleppo, Syria, sender asks addressee to cable
authorization from British Consul to visa EDWARD AZRAK's Panamanian
passport for six months in Syria as a tourist. In view of the fact
that EDWARD has not been able to obtain a visa for the United States
at this Embassy it is possible he will attempt entry to the United
States from Syria."
The last paragraph of the above-mentioned despatch is quoted
below:
"In view of the close comections which various members
of the AZRAK family had with the Japanese before the war
(through their representatives they could even be said to have
continued to have business cornections with the Japanese since
the outbreak of the WAR) and in view of the recommendations of
local Canal Zone Intelligence agencies that visas be denied
to members of this family, the Embassy has been reluctant to
issue them visas. The request which was made of members of
the AZRAK family for completion of sponsorship procedure was
based upon the material appearing in this despatch. Until such
time as it is believed that the AZRAKS could not longer present
any threat to the United States' war effort, it is this Embassy's
opinion that visas should not be granted to any member of this
family without the submission of sponsorship forms and an
advisory approval from the Department."
In October 1945 the Embassy at Panama was informed of the Depart-
mentsdisapproval of Edward Azrak's application for a visa. However,
in March 1946 the Department, though disapproving the assuance of visas
to Alberto and Victor Azrak, left the decision in Edward's case to the
discretion of the Mission because it had been represented that Edward
was ill and needed to come to the United States for medical attention.
On March 14, 1946 a non-immigrant visa was issued to Edward Azrak.

Although visitors visas for entry into the United
States have been issued to members of the Asrak family
residing in Panama, the American Embassy there was for
a time reluctant to issue them in view of the recommenda-
tions of local Canal Zone intelligence agencies and the
close connections which various members of the Asrak
family had with the Japanese before the war. The Embasay
at Panama has stated that through their representatives
the family could even be said to have continued their
business connections with the Japanese after the outbreak
of the war. Subsequent statements from the Embassy. in-
dicate, however, that past suspicion regarding the family
may have originated largely in the sharp and occasionally
unethical methods smployed by them in their business
affairs. It appears thet intelligence agencies in the
Canal Zone with which the Embassy has conferred are of
the same opinion.

information contained in the last two sentences above was inadvertently
omitted from this Department's previous communications regarding

Form AD-1
Alien Departure Permit
Application No.24.218
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ALIEN'S APPLICATION FOR PERMISSION TO DEPART FROM
THE UNITED STATES
(For use by all aliens who desire to depart from the United States)
No fee prescribed
Send no money
This application must be executed in quintuplicate and sworn to before an officer
authorized to administer oaths. Each blank space must be filled in. If the space provided
is not suflicient to include the information requested, additional sheets of paper may be
utilized, with a reference to the question answered.
Five unmounted photographs of the applicant, recently made, not smaller than 2 by 2
nor larger than 3 by 3 inches, must be furnished with this application; one photograph to
be firmly afixed to each copy of the application in the blank space provided therefor. Each
photograph must be signed by the applicant in such manner as not to obscure the features.
An applicant registered or enrolled, or subject to registration or enrollment, for mili-
tary service in the United States must submit with this application a statement from the
Secretary of War or from his local draft board stating whether there are any objections to
his departure from the United States.
Other evidence may be required by the Secretary of State concerning the facts in the
applicant's case before a conclusion is reached on the application.
The receipt of the application will be acknowledged, and the applicant will be notified
later concerning the decision reached upon the application. If the permit to depart is
granted it will be forwarded to the appropriate authorities at the port through which the
applicant intends to depart from the United States, and the application number will appear
on a notification card which will be sent to the applicant. By calling upon the port author-
ities prior to or at the time of departure and presenting the notification card and identify-
ing himself as the person to whom permission to depart has been granted by the Secretary
of State, the applicant may be permitted to depart from the United States through the port
specified.
This application should be filed at least 30 days before the applicant intends to depart.
Any permit issued upon the basis of this application is subject to revocation at any time.
Every question must be answered in some manner, as no application will be acted upon
until complete information is furnished and unless it appears that the applicant has been
granted permission to enter the country of his destination and to pass through all countries
en route.

894.
On August 29, 1947, Miss Wolberg telephoned the Irish Legation/
regarding the underlying note of August 19, 1947 inquiring whether
111/8-1947
permission has been granted for the Reverend Joseph Francis O'Brien,
an Irish national, to travel to Japan. In the absence of Mr. Skentel-
bery with whom she had spoken previously on this case, she spoke to
Nrs. Stone who seemed to be familiar with the note of August 19th.
Miss Wolberg explained that on August 8, 1947 the Department had sent
an airgram to the American Consulate at Dublin confirming the fact
that permission had been granted for Father O'Brien to travel to
Japan and instructing the Consul to give this information to the Irish
Department of External Affairs, together with other information re-
garding his documentation. Miss Wolberg further explained that she
had previously acquainted Er. Skentelbery with the case. Mrs. Stone
thanked Miss Wolberg for this information and stated that she would
give it to the Chargé. She further stated that this conversation
could be considered an acknowledgment to the Legation's note.

DENISE ANNE BICKART, being duly sworn, desposes and says:
That she is a French citizen and resident of 21 Shioya, mear Kobe,
Japan where she has resided for about 10 years including internment by
the Japanese during the recent war.
That she is making this to the Military Authorities to enable her
entering Japan for the purpose of joining her husband, Rene Biekart, a
French citizen, businesaman and resident of 21 Shioya, near Kobe, Japan.
That she left Jepan in October 1945 for the purpose of recovering
her health in Europe which had been seriously impared during the affi-
ent's internment in a Japanese concentration camp.
That presently she is staying with her father-in-law, Mr. Isidore
Bickart, naturalized American citizen, at 295 Central Park West, New
York City.
That any consideration that may be shown by the Allied Military
Authorities in granting permission of affiant's return to Japan will be
greatly appreciated.
Sevnire anne Brichart:

=== Page 492 === (confidence 0.99)
Form AD-1
Alien Departure Permit
Application No. 24718
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ALIEN'S APPLICATION FOR PERMISSION TO DEPART FROM
THE UNITED STATES
(For use by all aliens who desire to depart from the United States)
No fee prescribed
Send no money
This application must be executed in quintuplicate and sworn to before an officer
authorized to administer oaths. Each blank space must be filled in. If the space provided
is not sufficient to include the information requested, additional sheets of paper may be
utilized, with a reference to the question answered.
Five unmounted photographs of the applicant, recently made, not smaller than 2 by 2
nor larger than 3 by 3 inches, must be furnished with this application; one photograph to
be firmly affixed to each copy of the application in the blank space provided therefor. Each
photograph must be signed by the applicant in such manner as not to obscure the features.
An applicant registered or enrolled, or subject to registration or enrollment, for mili-
tary service in the United States must submit with this application a statement from the
Secretary of War or from his local draft board stating whether there are any objections to
his departure from the United States.
Other evidence may be required by the Secretary of State concerning the facts in the
applicant's case before a conclusion is reached on the application.
The receipt of the application will be acknowledged, and the applicant will be notified
later concerning the decision reached upon the application. If the permit to depart is
granted it will be forwarded to the appropriate authorities at the port through which the
applicant intends to depart from the United States, and the application number will appear
on a notification card which will be sent to the applicant. By calling upon the port author-
ities prior to or at the time of departure and presenting the notification card and identify-
ing himself as the person to whom permission to depart has been granted by the Secretary
of State, the applicant may be permitted to depart from the United States through the port
specified.
This application should be filed at least 30 days before the applicant intends to depart.
Any permit issued upon the basis of this application is subject to revocation at any time.
Every question must be answered in some manner, as no application will be acted upon
until complete information is furnished and unless it appears that the applicant has been
granted permission to enter the country of his destination and to pass through all countries
en route.

=== Page 502 === (confidence 0.95)
De/R
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
VISA DIVISION
MEMORANDUM
TO:
NN
- Mr. Meyer
- Mr. Hutchison
FROM: VD - Mr. Alexander
1. I have some doubt concerning the correctness
of the third and fourth paragraphs of the draft letter
to Senator Langer and the second and third paragraphs
of the draft letter to Mr. Ismail at Bombay.
2. Unless American consular officers have been
fully instructed regarding the procedure they should
follow in cases of this kind (and I find no copy of
such instructions attached) they will not know what to
do about such applications to them as are being suggested
in these letters.
3. I have some doubts as to whether it would be
more appropriate for Indian nationals to take up
applications for permission to enter Japan with the
American authorities instead of the British authorities.
It is true that the Supreme Allied Commander's Office
in Japan has said that in the cases of aliens whose
governments have no representation in Japan the applica-
tions should be taken up through diplomatic channels with
the Department of State. However, as I understand it,
India, although a self-governing nation, is still
member of the British Commonwealth of Nations, and as
11947
such they may have representation in Japan through the
British. I raise the question, therefore, whether it
could not be more appropriate for such cases to be taken
up through the British representatives in Japan than
FOORD
through the American-authorities.
Ten Unt
4. Ono
3.t.
08.
Diet

=== Page 503 === (confidence 0.94)
DC/R
-2-
4. One more thing I believe we must keep in mind.
Although General McArthur 1s an American General he 1s
acting for all the Allies in Japan, and his administration
is not exclusively an American operation. It is therefore
not necessary for us to handle all cases of this kind.
He has suggested it only if the alien's country is not
represented in Japan.
811.11l Ismai1, Haj1 M.
VD:RCAlexander:GER
PA

=== Page 504 === (confidence 0.94)
Delk

Liss Wolberg telephoned Cantain Keough to inquire whether the
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers had approved Mr. Rawnsley's
travel to Japan.
Captain Kecugh stated, after looking up the file, that Mr. Rawn-
sley's name is one of 80 which were recently transmitted to the
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers for approval under the quota
of businessmen allotted to the United States. The Supreme Commander
had not yet approved Nr. Rawnsley's entry into Japan because the
United States quota is presently filled. Cartain Keough stated that he
was trying to get SCAP tc approve the remaining names on the list of
80 but that to date he had not been able to do so. He said that
Miss Wolberg should check back in a few days to see whether approval
had been received as yet.

=== Page 536 === (confidence 0.99)
Form AD-1
Alien Departure Permit
Application No.
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ALIEN'S APPLICATION FOR PERMISSION TO DEPART FROM
THE UNITED STATES
(For use by all aliens who desire to depart from the United States)
No fee prescribed
Send no money
This application must be executed in quintuplicate and sworn to before an officer
authorized to administer oaths. Each blank space must be filled in. If the space provided
is not suflicient to include the information requested, additional sheets of paper may be
utilized, with a reference to the question answered.
Five unmounted photographs of the applicant, recently made, not smaller than 2 by 2
nor larger than 3 by 3 inches, must be furnished with this application; one photograph to
be firmly affixed to each copy of the application in the blank space provided therefor. Each
photograph must be signed by the applicant in such manner as not to obscure the features.
An applicant registered or enrolled, or subject to registration or enrollment, for mili-
tary service in the United States must submit with this application a statement from the
Secretary of War or from his local draft board stating whether there are any objectiqns to
his departure from the United States.
Other evidence may be required by the Secretary of State concerning the facts in the
applicant's case before a conclusion is reached on the application.
The receipt of the application will be acknowledged, and the applicant will be notified
later concerning the decision reached upon the application. If the permit to depart is
granted it will be forwarded to the appropriate authorities at the port through which the
applicant intends to depart from the United States, and the application number will appear
on a notification card which will be sent to the applicant. By calling upon the port author-
ities prior to or at the time of departure and presenting the notification card and identify-
ing himself as the person to whom permission to depart has been granted by the Secretary
of State, the applicant may be permitted to depart from the United States through the port
specified.
This application should be filed at least 30 days before the applicant intends to depart.
Any permit issued upon the basis of this application is subject to revocation at any time.
Every question must be answered in some manner, as no application will be acted upon
until complete information is furnished and unless it appears that the applicant has been
granted permission to enter the country of his destination and to pass through all countries
en route.
THE DEPARTURE CONTROL OFFICER WILL FILL IN THIS SPACE

Departure Control Officer.

and inquired for Mr. O'Brien. She had
been talking with him yesterday about

Noura Trading Co., of New York. It
appears that he has been cleared by SCAP
for entry into Japan. He states Mrs.
Shipley informed he should get a pass-
port. Miss Eads thinks it was a form
letter from PD inquiring if these people
were going to use their Mil. Permit.
However, he is not an American citizen
having only taken out first papers. I
understand he want to the Iranian Con-
sulate in N.Y.
He has permission to enter Japan
between Oct. 16 and 30. Here 1s what 1s
troubling him. He wants to go to India
after visiting Japan and he is afraid
he might not be able to re-enter the
Us if he does so. I understand there
would be no question about his re-entry
1f he went to Japan only.
As Mr. O'Brien went home ill I
told Miss Eads I would write up the
information she had given me and present
1t to you.
